⚠️ *The figure is a ranked data panel with a schematic choropleth. The choropleth carries no projection, scale or true coastal shape; it orders the coastal units and shades them by erosion share. Every figure on it is from the cited official document and carries that document's date.*

### 1. The intuition, before any terminology

⚠️ Ask how long India's coastline is and you will get three different official answers, all current, all correct. ⚠️ **This is not a scandal and it is not a contradiction. It is a consequence of what a coastline is.**

⚠️ Recall L1: a coast is a **zone**, the shoreline **moves continuously**, and a coastline is a **cartographic line drawn to represent the boundary at a stated datum and resolution**. ❗ **A coastline length is therefore a property of the measuring rule, not of the coast.** ⚠️ Change the datum, change the resolution of the source imagery, change the treatment of creeks, inlets and islands — and the number changes without a single grain of sand moving. ⚠️ Finer resolution resolves more indentation, and more indentation is more length.

❗ **The examinable consequence is a discipline:** **never state an Indian coastline length without its series, its authority and its date, and never mix two series inside one sentence.**

### 2. The three series, reconciled

| Series | Length | Authority and purpose | Date carried |
|---|---|---|---|
| 📰 **A** | **6,907.18 km** | **National Centre for Coastal Research** (Ministry of Earth Sciences) — the length **analysed** in the National Assessment of Shoreline Changes, 1990-2018 | Reported in **RS Unstarred Q.2117, answered 18.12.2025** |
| 📰 **B** | **7,516.6 km** | The **legacy Survey of India** series; the "old coastline" column of the 2025 re-assessment | Reported in **PIB Release ID 2198800, 04 DEC 2025** |
| 📰 **C** | **11,098.81 km** | **National Hydrographic Office with the Survey of India**, on terms of reference from the **National Security Council Secretariat**, using GIS software and **high-resolution High-Water Line (HWL)** data; accepted by the **Coastal Protection and Development Advisory Committee** after concurrence from all coastal States and Union Territories; promulgated by a **Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways circular dated 29 April 2025** | Reported in **PIB Release ID 2198800, 04 DEC 2025** |

📰 **The revision arithmetic, verified this generation.** ⚠️ Both columns of the state table were re-summed and reproduce the stated totals with **zero residual**: the legacy column to **7,516.60** against a stated 7,516.6, and the revised column to **11,098.81** against a stated 11,098.81. ⚠️ The increase is **+3,582.21 km, or +47.66 per cent**. ⚠️ The largest multiplier is **West Bengal at 4.58 times** (157.5 → 721.02 km) and the smallest is **Kerala at 1.05 times** (569.7 → 600.15 km).

❌ **[N-03] Khullar's "about 6,100 km" is obsolete and is used only as a dated contrast.** ✅ Khullar, printed folio 49 (local scan PDF p.41): *"India has about 6100 km long coast most of which is more or less uniform and regular with a few creeks, inlets, back waters and promonotories here and there."* ❗ **That is a book-era figure.** ⚠️ It is useful **only** as a demonstration of the lesson's own thesis: four numbers now exist for one coast — a book-era 6,100 km, an analytical 6,907.18 km, an administrative 7,516.6 km and a re-assessed 11,098.81 km — and the spread between them is a spread of **method**, not of coast. ❗ **No coastline length appears bare anywhere in this edition.**

❌ **[N-17] The legacy column carries no Daman & Diu row at all, and still closes exactly.** ⚠️ Daman & Diu appears in the revised column at **54.38 km** with **no legacy entry**, yet the legacy column still sums exactly to **7,516.60 km**. ❗ **The legacy national figure therefore never contained a separate Daman & Diu entry.** ❗ **State this if the table is reproduced; do not silently insert a value to make the row look complete.**

❌ **[N-12] The release's ranking sentence is universe-dependent.** 📰 The release states that *"the length of Andhra Pradesh's coastline has become third in position."* ⚠️ Tested against both columns: counting **States only**, Andhra Pradesh moves from **2nd to 3rd**, overtaken by Tamil Nadu — so the claim holds. ⚠️ Counting **States and Union Territories together**, Andhra Pradesh moves from **3rd to 4th**, behind Andaman & Nicobar, Gujarat and Tamil Nadu — so the claim does not hold. ❗ **Any ranking sentence must name its counting universe.** ⚠️ The safe form is: *"on a states-only ranking Andhra Pradesh moves from second to third; including Union Territories it moves from third to fourth."*

### 3. [N-14] The measurement line is not the regulatory line — stated here, completed at L16

❗ **This is the single highest-value trap in the topic and it is unobtainable from any local book.**

📰 **PIB Release ID 2198800, 04 DEC 2025**, states that the revised measurement used **high-resolution data of the High-Water Line (HWL)**, and then states, in terms, that *"the revised coastline length of 11,098.81 km **does not affect areas covered under the CRZ regulations, as CRZ boundaries are determined solely on the basis of the actual High Tide Line (HTL)** demarcated by the National Centre for Sustainable Coastal Management (NCSCM), Chennai, under the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change,"* adding that the HTL mapping used **very high-resolution digital aerial photographs from the Survey of India, validated through detailed ground verification**, and that *"the updated coastline measurement has **no bearing on CRZ jurisdiction or its regulatory framework**."*

❗ **Two lines, two agencies, two purposes — on the release of 04 DEC 2025 and the notification of 18 January 2019:**

| | **HWL — High-Water Line** | **HTL — High Tide Line** |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Used for | **Measuring** coastline length | **Defining** the Coastal Regulation Zone |
| ⚠️ Demarcated by | **National Hydrographic Office** with the Survey of India | **NCSCM, Chennai**, under the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change |
| ⚠️ Method | GIS with high-resolution HWL data | Very high-resolution aerial photography, ground-verified |
| ⚠️ Consequence | A **47.66 per cent longer** national coastline | ❗ **Unchanged** CRZ areas and jurisdiction |

❗ **Therefore: on the 04 DEC 2025 release, India's coastline is now officially 47.66 per cent longer and not one square metre of Coastal Regulation Zone has changed.** ⚠️ Stating that sentence, with the ministry's own reason attached, is the strongest single move available on this topic. 🔁 The legal architecture that rests on the HTL is the whole of **L16**.

### 4. [N-02] The shoreline-change record, corrected

📰 **Rajya Sabha Unstarred Question No. 2117, answered 18.12.2025 (MoEFCC):** the NCCR *"has undertaken a comprehensive scientific study on National Assessment of Shoreline Changes along Indian Coast for the period 1990-2018. The study is based on analysis of multi-spectral satellite images along with the field-surveyed data. Out of 6907.18 km of coastline studied by NCCR, it was estimated that **33.6 % is eroding, 39.6 % is stable and 26.8% is accreting**."*

❌ **[N-02] The canonical owner's figure is wrong against the answer of 18.12.2025, and is arithmetically self-refuting.** ✅ C1 §6 and C1's date/status control both give the accretion share as **one-tenth of a percentage point above the official value**, alongside 33.6 per cent eroding and 39.6 per cent stable. ⚠️ **That triple sums to 100.1 per cent**, which is impossible for an exhaustive partition of a single coastline. 📰 **The official triple is 33.6 / 39.6 / 26.8, which closes to exactly 100.0.** ❗ **The accretion share is 26.8 per cent, and the owner's inflated value is corrected and does not appear anywhere in this edition.**

📰 **The national component lengths**, from the same answer of 18.12.2025: **eroding 2,318.31 km · stable 2,733.86 km · accreting 1,855.03 km**, of **6,907.18 km** studied. ⚠️ All four column sums were re-verified this generation and reproduce the stated totals exactly.

❌ **[N-15] A rounding residual is disclosed rather than "fixed."** ⚠️ In the table of 18.12.2025 the three component lengths sum to **6,907.20 km** against a stated total of **6,907.18 km** — a **0.02 km** discrepancy. ❗ **This is a rounding artefact in the published table, not an error, and it is disclosed whenever the components are shown.** ❗ **Never adjust a published figure to make a column close.**

❗ **Two handling rules that travel with every use of these numbers.** ❗ **They are classes over a 28-year window, 1990-2018 — not annual rates.** C1's existing caution on this point is correct and stands. ❗ **They refer to Series A (6,907.18 km), which is neither Series B nor Series C.**

❗ **The trap, stated as sharply as it can be, on the table of 18.12.2025:** 📰 **the state with the largest eroding LENGTH is Gujarat, at 537.5 km. The state with the largest eroding SHARE is West Bengal, at 60.5 per cent.** ⚠️ Gujarat's share is only **27.6 per cent**, below the national average, because it has by far the longest studied coastline; West Bengal's eroding length is **323.07 km**, third in the country, on a studied coast of only 534.35 km.

❗ **Length and share rank differently and must never be interchanged.** ⚠️ The two measures answer different questions: **length** answers *where is the most coast eroding?* — which is the resource-allocation question; **share** answers *which coast is most affected relative to its own extent?* — which is the vulnerability question. ⚠️ A sentence that says "Gujarat is the worst affected" is defensible on length and indefensible on share, and vice versa for West Bengal. ❗ **Say which measure you are using.**

⚠️ **Three further readings from the same table, each worth a line in an answer.**

- 📰 **The highest accretion shares — Odisha 51.0 per cent and Andhra Pradesh 49.6 per cent — are both on the east coast**, the same coast that is proportionally the most erosive. ⚠️ A single coast is simultaneously building and retreating in different reaches, which is exactly what the sediment-cell model predicts (L5).
- 📰 **The lowest erosion shares are Goa at 19.2 per cent, Karnataka at 23.7 per cent and Maharashtra at 25.5 per cent** — three contiguous west-coast units, and the only three units where the **stable** share exceeds 50 per cent by a wide margin.
- ⚠️ **"Stable" does not mean static.** It means that no net change exceeded the detection threshold over the window; sediment was moving throughout (L5).

📰 **One dated mention of scale, used once and not built into a trend.** From part (c) of the same answer: the **National Coastal Mission** year-wise expenditure or allocation, in rupees crore, was **2021-22: 27.79 · 2022-23: 16.38 · 2023-24: 32.44 · 2024-25: 7.91 · 2025-26: 7.5**, the last *"including allocation from extended National Coastal Mission Scheme"*, all as stated on **18.12.2025**. ❗ **This is quoted once, for scale, with its date attached.** ❗ **No trend argument is built on five noisy annual figures**, and none should be.

### 6. [C1 causal control] Why a national statistic cannot explain a local reach

✅ **C1's causal control is the governing analytical sentence of this lesson:** *"sea-level rise alone does not explain a local erosion reach. **Sediment supply, dams, ports, sand mining, storms, currents, coastal structures, subsidence and measurement period interact**; hard protection can transfer erosion downdrift."*

⚠️ **Read against the table above, this has three consequences.**

1. ⚠️ **A national erosion percentage is an aggregate of cell-level budgets, and the budgets have different causes.** The same 33.6 per cent of the 18.12.2025 table contains reaches starved by an upstream dam, reaches starved by a port breakwater, reaches losing sand to extraction, reaches subsiding, and reaches simply migrating landward. ❗ **No single cause can be read off the aggregate.**
2. ⚠️ **Erosion and accretion in the same table are partly ONE process counted twice with opposite signs.** Where a structure traps drift, the updrift reach registers as accreting and the downdrift reach as eroding. ❗ **Reporting them as two independent phenomena conceals the mechanism.**
3. ⚠️ **The measurement period is a variable, not a background condition.** A 28-year window averages over seasonal reversals, storm events and the construction dates of structures. ❗ **A reach reported stable over 1990-2018 may have been eroding since 2010.**

🔁 **The routing, stated in full and by exact path, because this is where the edition's largest boundary sits.** ❗ **The 2022 General Studies Paper III, Question 18 demand — *causes, effects and management techniques for coastal erosion in India*, 15 marks and 250 words — is the largest coastal Mains demand in the ledgers, and it is OWNED BY `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24_Coastal-and-Marine-Ecology-CRZ-Blue-Economy.md`.** ❗ **Topic 10 does not own it and does not attempt it.** ⚠️ What Topic 10 contributes is the **physical mechanism and the evidential discipline**: wave energy and lithology (L2), the sediment cell and downdrift transfer (L5), relative sea level and subsidence (L10), surge (L12), and the measurement conventions taught here. 🔁 **Coastal sand mining (2019 GS-III Q7), marine pollution and oil spills (2023 GS-III Q8), mangrove depletion (2019 GS-I Q5) and marine dead zones (2018 GS-I Q7) are routed to the same owner.** ❗ **No option, key or elimination is given for any objective demand named here.**

### 7. Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What this lesson's claim explains | What it does not explain |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Series-dependence of coastline length | Why three official numbers coexist; why a finer survey yields a longer coast; why the figures are not interchangeable | Which series a third-party source has used, which must be read off that source |
| ❗ The HWL/HTL separation | Why the 47.66 per cent longer coastline of 04 DEC 2025 changes no regulatory area; why the two lines have different agencies and purposes | The content of the regulation itself, which is L16 |
| 📰 The shoreline-change table | The national pattern, the state pattern and the length-versus-share distinction | The **cause** in any reach, which requires a cell-level analysis no retrieved source provides for India |

**Strongest objection.** ⚠️ If a coastline length is an artefact of measurement resolution, then the whole exercise is arbitrary — the "true" length is undefined, and the 11,098.81 km figure of 04 DEC 2025 is no more real than the legacy 7,516.6 km one. Reporting it as an increase is therefore misleading.

**Reply.** ⚠️ The first half of the objection is correct and is this lesson's own thesis; the second half does not follow. ⚠️ A measured length is **not arbitrary** — it is **rule-dependent and reproducible**, which is a different thing. ⚠️ Given a stated datum, resolution and treatment of creeks and islands, the measurement is determinate and repeatable, and 📰 the release documents exactly those choices: the High-Water Line, high-resolution survey data, GIS processing, and acceptance after concurrence from every coastal State and Union Territory. ❗ **What is misleading is not the number but an uncontextualised comparison**, and the correct formulation is a statement about method: *"on the legacy Survey of India series India's coastline measured 7,516.6 km; re-measured in 2025 at higher resolution against the High-Water Line it measures 11,098.81 km. The increase of 3,582.21 km reflects a change of method and resolution, not of coast."* ⚠️ The ministry's own CRZ sentence is the proof that the official system treats it that way too.

❓ **Residual, which this edition does not close.** ❓ **No littoral-cell delineation for India was retrieved**, so the cell-level explanation the data demand cannot be performed here for any reach. ❓ **The era of Khullar's 6,100 km figure is not established (U-15).** ❓ **No post-2018 shoreline-change update was retrieved**, so nothing is said about change since the NCCR window closed. ❓ **The reason the NCCR study length (6,907.18 km) differs from the legacy administrative figure (7,516.6 km) is not stated in the retrieved documents**, and this edition does **not** speculate about it.

- **Syllabus:** ⚠️ *"Changes in critical geographical features … and the effects of such changes"* (General Studies Paper I) — shoreline change is that clause's most directly measured Indian expression — and the **PARTIAL** General Studies Paper III disaster clause, discharged as exposure evidence.
- **Verified PYQ:** 🔁 No owned demand terminates here. ❗ **2022 GS-III Q18 (coastal erosion in India, 15/250) is Environment-owned — `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24_Coastal-and-Marine-Ecology-CRZ-Blue-Economy.md` — and is named, routed and not attempted.** ⚠️ This lesson is also the **evidence base** for the hazard half of the owned demand **O2**, assembled at L18.
- **Traps:** ❗ **Mixing two coastline series in one sentence.** ⚠️ Writing a coastline length without its series, authority and date. ⚠️ Repeating the owner's inflated accretion share. ⚠️ Reading the NCCR classes as **annual rates**. ⚠️ Interchanging eroding **length** and eroding **share**. ⚠️ Stating a ranking without naming the counting universe. ⚠️ Inserting a Daman & Diu figure into the legacy column. ⚠️ "Fixing" the 0.02 km rounding residual. ⚠️ Treating "stable" as "static". ⚠️ Building a trend from five annual allocation figures.
- **Answer use:** ❗ Lead a measurement question with the **rule-dependence** sentence, then give the series. ⚠️ *"A coastline length is a property of the measuring rule — the datum, the resolution and the treatment of creeks and islands — which is why three official Indian figures coexist: 6,907.18 km analysed by the National Centre for Coastal Research for 1990-2018; 7,516.6 km on the legacy Survey of India series; and 11,098.81 km on the 2025 re-assessment against the High-Water Line, reported on 04 DEC 2025. Decisively, that last revision does not alter Coastal Regulation Zone areas, because those rest on the High Tide Line demarcated by NCSCM."*

Revision notes:

- ❗ **A coastline length is a property of the MEASURING RULE, not of the coast** — datum, resolution, treatment of creeks and islands. ❗ **Never mix two series in one sentence.**
- 📰 **Series A — 6,907.18 km**, NCCR study baseline, 1990-2018 (RS Q.2117, answered **18.12.2025**). 📰 **Series B — 7,516.6 km**, legacy Survey of India series (PIB **2198800**, **04 DEC 2025**).
- 📰 **Series C — 11,098.81 km**, 2025 re-assessment by the **National Hydrographic Office with the Survey of India**, terms of reference from the **National Security Council Secretariat**, **HWL** data and GIS, accepted by **CPDAC** after concurrence from all coastal States/UTs, promulgated by **MoPSW circular of 29 April 2025** (PIB **2198800**, **04 DEC 2025**). 📰 **B → C: +3,582.21 km, +47.66 %.** ⚠️ Both columns re-summed with **zero residual**. ⚠️ **Largest multiplier West Bengal 4.58× (157.5 → 721.02); smallest Kerala 1.05×.**
- ❌ **[N-17]** The legacy column has **no Daman & Diu row** and still closes exactly. ❗ **Do not insert one.** ❌ **[N-12]** *"Andhra Pradesh… third in position"* holds **only on a states-only ranking** (2nd → 3rd). **Including UTs, AP moves 3rd → 4th.** ❗ **Name the counting universe.**
- ❗ **[N-14] HWL ≠ HTL.** 📰 The revision *"does not affect areas covered under the CRZ regulations, as CRZ boundaries are determined solely on the basis of the actual High Tide Line (HTL) demarcated by NCSCM, Chennai"* (04 DEC 2025). ❗ **Coastline +47.66 %, CRZ area unchanged.** 🔁 L16.
- ❌ **[N-02]** C1's inflated accretion share makes its triple sum to **100.1 %**, which is **impossible**. 📰 **Official: 33.6 eroding / 39.6 stable / 26.8 accreting**, closing to **100.0**.
- 📰 **Component lengths:** eroding **2,318.31 km** · stable **2,733.86 km** · accreting **1,855.03 km**, of **6,907.18 km**. ❌ **[N-15]** Components sum to **6,907.20** against a stated **6,907.18** — a **0.02 km rounding artefact**. ❗ **Disclose; never "fix" a published figure.**
- ❗ **Classes over a 28-YEAR WINDOW (1990-2018), NOT annual rates.** ❗ They refer to **Series A only**.
- ❗ **THE CORE TRAP:** largest eroding **LENGTH** = **Gujarat 537.5 km**; largest eroding **SHARE** = **West Bengal 60.5 %**. ⚠️ Gujarat's share is only **27.6 %**. ❗ **Length answers "where is most coast eroding"; share answers "which coast is most affected relative to itself."**
- 📰 **Proportional comparison:** west coast **3,762.62 km**, eroding over **29.6 %**; east coast **3,144.56 km**, eroding over **38.3 %**. ❗ **The east is proportionally more erosive** 🔁 L14. 📰 **Highest accretion: Odisha 51.0 %, Andhra Pradesh 49.6 %** — both east coast. ⚠️ **Lowest erosion: Goa 19.2 %, Karnataka 23.7 %, Maharashtra 25.5 %.**
- ⚠️ **"Stable" ≠ static** — only that no net change exceeded the detection threshold. 📰 **National Coastal Mission (Rs crore), as on 18.12.2025:** 2021-22 **27.79** · 2022-23 **16.38** · 2023-24 **32.44** · 2024-25 **7.91** · 2025-26 **7.5** (including the extended scheme). ❗ **Scale only; no trend argument.**
- ✅ **C1 causal control:** *"sediment supply, dams, ports, sand mining, storms, currents, coastal structures, subsidence and measurement period interact; hard protection can transfer erosion downdrift."* ❗ **Erosion and accretion in one table are partly ONE process counted twice.**
- ❌ **[N-03]** ✅ Khullar's *"about 6100 km"* (printed folio 49, local scan PDF p.41) is **book-era**, used only as a dated contrast. ❗ **[N-16]** His contents page cites the section at 45 while the heading was read on printed **49**; ❗ **cite the render, never the contents page.**
- 🔁 **ROUTED OUT, by exact path:** ❗ **2022 GS-III Q18, coastal erosion in India (15/250) → `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24_Coastal-and-Marine-Ecology-CRZ-Blue-Economy.md`** — the largest coastal Mains demand in the ledgers, **and it is not ours**. 🔁 Sand mining, marine pollution, oil spills, mangrove depletion, dead zones → the same owner.
- 🔑 **Mnemonic for the three series: A-B-C = Analysed, Administrative, Current re-assessment** — 6,907.18 · 7,516.6 · 11,098.81.

### Concept check

**Question:** A newspaper reports, naming neither its series nor its document dates: "India's coastline has grown by nearly 3,600 km, and with 33.6% of it eroding and Gujarat the worst affected state, the area under coastal regulation will expand sharply." Identify every error in that sentence and write a corrected version.

**Model answer:** There are **four errors and they compound**. First, the sentence **mixes two series**. The increase from 7,516.6 km to 11,098.81 km is a re-measurement of the legacy Survey of India figure by the National Hydrographic Office with the Survey of India, reported on 04 DEC 2025; the 33.6 per cent eroding figure belongs to a **different series**, the 6,907.18 km analysed by the National Centre for Coastal Research for 1990-2018 and reported on 18.12.2025. The percentage cannot be applied to the revised length. Second, "has grown" misdescribes the change: the coast did not grow, the **measurement method and resolution changed** — higher-resolution data against the High-Water Line resolves more indentation, and more indentation is more length. Third, **"Gujarat the worst affected" is ambiguous and is false on one reading**. Gujarat has the largest eroding **length**, 537.5 km, but its eroding **share** is 27.6 per cent, below the national average; the largest eroding share is **West Bengal at 60.5 per cent**. The measure must be named. Fourth, and most consequentially, **the regulatory inference is contradicted by the issuing ministry**, which states that the revised length *"does not affect areas covered under the CRZ regulations, as CRZ boundaries are determined solely on the basis of the actual High Tide Line (HTL) demarcated by NCSCM, Chennai."* The measurement line is the **HWL**; the regulatory line is the **HTL**; they are different lines demarcated by different agencies for different purposes. A corrected version: *"India's coastline has been re-measured from 7,516.6 km to 11,098.81 km using high-resolution High-Water Line data (04 DEC 2025), a change of method rather than of coast. Separately, of the 6,907.18 km assessed by the National Centre for Coastal Research for 1990-2018, 33.6 per cent was classed as eroding, with Gujarat recording the largest eroding length and West Bengal the largest eroding share (18.12.2025). The re-measurement does not alter Coastal Regulation Zone areas, which rest on the High Tide Line."*

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a percentage from one official series as applicable to the length of another. Each percentage is a share of its own denominator, and the three Indian coastline series have different denominators, different authorities, different methods and different purposes. Transplanting a share between them manufactures a figure that no source supports.

**Original Mains practice [20 marks]:** Three official figures for the length of India's coastline are current simultaneously. Analyse why, and examine what the national shoreline-change assessment does and does not establish. *(Analyse and examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model (about 250 words):** A coastline length is a property of the measuring rule rather than of the coast: the datum chosen, the resolution of the source data, and the treatment of creeks, inlets and islands all determine it, and finer resolution resolves more indentation and therefore more length. Three Indian series coexist. The National Centre for Coastal Research analysed **6,907.18 km** for its shoreline-change study of 1990-2018. The legacy Survey of India series gives **7,516.6 km**. The 2025 re-assessment by the National Hydrographic Office with the Survey of India, using high-resolution High-Water Line data and GIS, gives **11,098.81 km**, an increase of 3,582.21 km or 47.66 per cent, reported on 04 DEC 2025. Decisively, that revision **does not alter Coastal Regulation Zone areas**, because those rest on the High Tide Line demarcated by NCSCM — a different line, agency and purpose.

The shoreline assessment, reported on 18.12.2025, establishes that of the 6,907.18 km studied, **33.6 per cent was eroding, 39.6 per cent stable and 26.8 per cent accreting**, with Gujarat recording the largest eroding length at 537.5 km and West Bengal the largest eroding share at 60.5 per cent. Length and share therefore rank differently and are not interchangeable.

It does not establish causation. The classes are aggregates over a twenty-eight-year window, not annual rates; erosion and accretion within one table are partly a single redistribution counted twice, since structures accrete updrift and starve downdrift; and sediment supply, dams, ports, sand mining, storms, subsidence and the measurement period all interact at cell scale.

**Rubric (quantified, 20 marks):** 6 for reconciling all three series with the correct length, authority, purpose and document date for each, and with no two mixed in one claim; 5 for the method difference, requiring the rule-dependence argument and the explicit HWL-versus-HTL separation with the ministry's own CRZ statement; 5 for the erosion pattern — the corrected national triple closing to 100.0, the component lengths, and at least two state-level readings; 4 for the length-versus-share trap, naming Gujarat on length and West Bengal on share and explaining why the two rank differently. Deduct 3 for applying a percentage from one series to the length of another, 3 for stating that the revision expands or alters CRZ areas, 2 for quoting the owner's inflated accretion share, and 2 for presenting the classes as annual rates.

⚠️ *The figure is a legal-zonation schematic with an exclusion index. Horizontal distances are not to scale; the categories are shown in their definitional relation, not in any real geographical configuration. All provisions are read from the gazette text itself.*

### 1. The intuition, before any terminology

⚠️ Lesson 1 established that a coast is a **zone** and that the water's edge has no single position. ⚠️ Lesson 15 established that **three official coastline lengths coexist**, all correct, because length depends on the measuring rule.

❗ **Now put a lawyer in front of that coast and the problem becomes acute.** ⚠️ A regulation cannot say "do not build near the sea", because "near the sea" is not a location. ⚠️ **It must nominate one line, define that line precisely, name a single agency to demarcate it, and then measure distances from it.** ⚠️ Everything in this lesson follows from that necessity: the choice of the **spring-tide high-water line**, the single demarcating agency, the fixed distances, the map scale, and the special rule for creeks where the "sea front" rule would make no sense.

❗ **The decisive consequence is that the regulatory line is not the measurement line.** ⚠️ India re-measured its coastline against the **High-Water Line** and, on the release of **04 DEC 2025**, got a figure **47.66 per cent larger**; the Coastal Regulation Zone rests on the **High Tide Line**, demarcated by a different agency for a different purpose, and **nothing regulated changed.**

### 2. [N-01] The citation correction, made first because the canonical owner carries the error

❌ **C1's date/status control cites the 2019 notification under the S.O. series, as number 37(E) of 18 January 2019.** ❗ **That is wrong.**

📰 **The gazette itself reads:** *"MINISTRY OF ENVIRONMENT, FOREST AND CLIMATE CHANGE — NOTIFICATION — New Delhi, the 18th January, 2019 — **G.S.R. 37(E)**"*, printed in **THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY, PART II—SEC. 3(i)**.

⚠️ **Where the error came from is itself instructive.** 📰 The same gazette records that it supersedes *"the Coastal Regulation Zone Notification 2011, number **S.O. 19(E)**, dated the 6th January, 2011."* ❗ **The S.O. series belongs to the superseded 2011 instrument; the series letter has evidently been carried forward onto its replacement.** ⚠️ Both notifications issue under **section 3 of the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986**, and 📰 the 2019 instrument records that it follows the examination of the report of a committee chaired by **Dr Shailesh Nayak** and a draft notification hosted on **18 April 2018**.

❗ **The corrected citation, to be used everywhere:** **Coastal Regulation Zone Notification 2019, `G.S.R. 37(E)`, dated 18 January 2019, Gazette of India Extraordinary, Part II—Section 3(i).** ❗ **The S.O.-series form of this citation does not appear anywhere in this edition.**

❗ **U-01, flagged and not resolved.** ⚠️ **CRZ 2019 has been amended since 2019.** ⚠️ **This edition does not cite any amendment number**, because no amendment gazette was retrieved in primary form this generation, and because the parent instrument is a **G.S.R.** the series letter of the amendments cannot be assumed either. ❗ **Say that it has been amended; do not list the amendments.**

### 3. The scope and the two line definitions — the geographical core of the instrument

📰 **Scope, from the gazette.** The Central Government declares *"the coastal stretches of the country and the water area up to its territorial water limit, **excluding the islands of Andaman and Nicobar and Lakshadweep and the marine areas surrounding these islands**"* as the Coastal Regulation Zone.

📰 **The landward extent and the two rules.**

| Setting | Landward extent of the CRZ |
|---|---|
| 📰 **Along the sea front** | *"The land area from **High Tide Line (HTL) to 500 meters** on the landward side."* |
| 📰 **Along tidal-influenced water bodies connected to the sea** | *"the land area between **HTL to 50 meters or width of the creek, whichever is less**"* — ⚠️ with the proviso that **until the Coastal Zone Management Plan is approved, the limit of 100 metres or the creek width, whichever is less, continues to apply** |
| 📰 **Intertidal zone** | *"the land area between the HTL and the **Low Tide Line (LTL)**"* |
| 📰 **Water area** | The water and bed area between LTL and the **territorial water limit (12 Nm)** in the sea, and between LTL on one bank and LTL on the opposite bank of a tidal-influenced water body |

📰 **The HTL, defined in the instrument itself:** *"the HTL means the line on the land upto which the **highest water line reaches during the spring tide**, as demarcated by the **National Centre for Sustainable Coastal Management (NCSCM)** in accordance with the laid down procedures and made available to various coastal States and Union territories."*

📰 **The extent of tidal influence, defined quantitatively:** it *"shall be determined based on **salinity concentration of five parts per thousand (ppt) measured during the driest period of the year**"*, and must be identified and demarcated in the Coastal Zone Management Plan. 📰 *"Tidal influenced water bodies"* are defined as *"the water bodies influenced by tidal effects from sea in the bays, estuaries, rivers, creeks, backwaters, lagoons, ponds that are connected to the sea."*

❗ **Three observations that convert this from legal detail into geography, and that are what an examiner can reward.**

1. ❗ **The HTL is a spring-tide line, not an average.** ⚠️ It is the **highest** of the high waters, which is a deliberately conservative choice: it places the regulatory boundary at the landward limit of normal tidal reach rather than at a mean position. 🔁 Recall L1: on a macrotidal, low-gradient coast like the Gulf of Khambhat the horizontal difference between a mean and a spring-tide line is large.
2. ❗ **Tidal influence is defined by salinity, not by distance.** ⚠️ Five parts per thousand **measured in the driest period** — that is, when fresh-water discharge is at its minimum and the salt wedge is furthest inland, which again is the conservative, maximum-extent choice. ⚠️ **A hydrological criterion is being used to fix a legal boundary**, which is a genuinely unusual and examinable piece of drafting.
3. ❗ **The creek rule exists because the sea-front rule would be absurd on a creek.** ⚠️ A 500 m band either side of a 60 m creek would regulate the whole landscape; **50 m or the creek width, whichever is less**, scales the regulation to the feature. ⚠️ **The instrument is making a scale decision**, which is exactly what C1's scale/map control says the topic requires.

### 4. [N-14 completed] HWL against HTL — the topic's sharpest discriminator

📰 **PIB Release ID 2198800, Ministry of Earth Sciences, 04 DEC 2025:** the 2025 coastline re-assessment used *"modern GIS software and high-resolution data of **High-Water Line (HWL)**"*; and *"the revised coastline length of 11,098.81 km **does not affect areas covered under the CRZ regulations, as CRZ boundaries are determined solely on the basis of the actual High Tide Line (HTL) demarcated by the National Centre for Sustainable Coastal Management (NCSCM), Chennai**, under the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change,"* with the HTL mapping carried out *"using very high-resolution digital aerial photographs from the Survey of India… subsequently validated through detailed ground verification. Therefore, the updated coastline measurement has **no bearing on CRZ jurisdiction or its regulatory framework**."*

📰 **The gazette itself closes the loop from the other side**, in Annexure-IV: *"Demarcation of High Tide Line (HTL) and Low Tide Line (LTL) **as carried out by NCSCM** for the entire coastline of the country, has been made available to the Coastal States or Union territories and **only such demarcation of HTL and LTL shall be applicable for all purposes of this notification**."*

❗ **Read together, the two documents establish a complete and unusually clean distinction:**

| | **HWL** | **HTL** |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Function | **Measurement** of coastline length | **Definition** of a regulatory boundary |
| ⚠️ Agency | **National Hydrographic Office** with the Survey of India | **NCSCM, Chennai**, under MoEFCC |
| ⚠️ Commissioned by | Terms of reference from the **National Security Council Secretariat** | The notification itself |
| ⚠️ Method | GIS with high-resolution HWL data | Very high-resolution aerial photography, ground-verified |
| ⚠️ Definitional anchor | The high-water line as surveyed | ❗ **The spring-tide highest water line**, by definition |
| ⚠️ Legal status | ⚠️ No regulatory effect | ❗ **The only demarcation valid for all purposes of the notification** |

❗ **The sentence to carry into an answer:** ⚠️ *"India's officially measured coastline grew by 47.66 per cent in 2025 and the area under coastal regulation did not change by a single metre, because the measurement rests on the High-Water Line while the regulation rests on the High Tide Line demarcated by NCSCM — two lines, two agencies, two purposes."*

### 5. The category architecture — delivered as a reference table, not narrated

❗ **U-11, the resolution stated explicitly.** ⚠️ The repository's answer-worthiness audit classes *"CRZ notification categories and shoreline-change series"* as **correctly Advanced and safely skippable**, while the owned Mains demand pulls coastal governance toward Core. ❗ **This edition's resolution: the zonation LOGIC is Core, because it is a geographical statement about how a coast is divided; the ENUMERATION is reference material and is not narrated.** ⚠️ The logic is four-fold — **ecologically sensitive, intertidal, urban, rural-by-density, and water** — and that structure is the part worth understanding.

📰 **The categories, verbatim in substance from `G.S.R. 37(E)`:**

| Category | Definition as notified |
|---|---|
| 📰 **CRZ-I A** | Ecologically sensitive areas and *"the geo-morphological features which play a role in maintaining the integrity of the coast"*: **mangroves** — *"in case mangrove area is more than **1000 square meters**, a buffer of **50 meters** along the mangroves shall be provided and such area shall also constitute CRZ–I A"* — **corals and coral reefs; sand dunes; biologically active mudflats; national parks, marine parks, sanctuaries, reserve forests, wildlife habitats and other protected areas under the Wild Life (Protection) Act, 1972, the Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980 or the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, including Biosphere Reserves; salt marshes; turtle nesting grounds; horse shoe crabs' habitats; sea grass beds; nesting grounds of birds; areas or structures of archaeological importance and heritage sites** |
| 📰 **CRZ-I B** | *"The intertidal zone i.e. the area between Low Tide Line and High Tide Line"* |
| 📰 **CRZ-II** | *"the developed land areas up to or close to the shoreline, within the existing municipal limits or in other existing legally designated urban areas, which are substantially built-up with a ratio of built-up plots to that of total plots being **more than 50 per cent** and have been provided with drainage and approach roads and other infrastructural facilities"* |
| 📰 **CRZ-III** | *"Land areas that are relatively undisturbed (viz. rural areas, etc.) and those which do not fall under CRZ-II"* |
| 📰 **CRZ-III A** | Densely populated CRZ-III areas, *"where the population density is **more than 2161 per square kilometre as per 2011 census base**"*; NDZ **50 m** from HTL, ❗ *"provided the CZMP… have been approved, **failing which, a NDZ of 200 meters shall continue to apply**"* |
| 📰 **CRZ-III B** | All other CRZ-III areas, density **less than 2161 per sq km** on the 2011 census base; NDZ **200 m** from HTL |
| 📰 **CRZ-IV A** | *"The water area and the sea bed area between the Low Tide Line up to **twelve nautical miles** on the seaward side"* |
| 📰 **CRZ-IV B** | The water and bed area of **tidal-influenced water bodies**, from the mouth up to the influence of tide, *"i.e., salinity of five parts per thousand (ppt) during the driest season of the year"* |

⚠️ **The geographical reading of this architecture, which is the part to actually learn:** ⚠️ **CRZ-I A is an overlay of ecological and geomorphological function** — it follows features, not lines; ⚠️ **CRZ-I B is a tidal band**; ⚠️ **CRZ-II and CRZ-III divide the land by degree of development, and CRZ-III then subdivides by population density on a fixed census base**; ⚠️ **CRZ-IV is water.** ❗ **One of the five criteria is ecological, one is tidal, one is built-form, one is demographic and one is hydrographic** — and noticing that the instrument mixes criteria of four different kinds is a better answer than reciting seven labels.

📰 **Areas requiring special consideration**, from paragraph 3.1: **Critically Vulnerable Coastal Areas**, naming the **Sundarban region of West Bengal** and other ecologically sensitive areas identified under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, *"such as **Gulf of Khambat and Gulf of Kutchh** in Gujarat, **Malvan, Achra-Ratnagiri** in Maharashtra, **Karwar**…"*. 🔁 Two of these are the very coasts taught at L1 and L13.

📰 **One line only on mangroves, as an adjacent Environment-owned anchor** (PIB Release ID 2146358, **21 JUL 2025**): the same release confirms the CRZ linkage captured above — mangroves are ecologically sensitive areas with a **50 m buffer where the mangrove area exceeds 1,000 sq m** — and records that **three times the number of mangroves lost must be replanted**. 🔁 **Mangrove ecology, restoration programmes including MISHTI, blue carbon and forest law are owned by `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24_Coastal-and-Marine-Ecology-CRZ-Blue-Economy.md`.**

### 6. [N-18] The no-development zone is conditional, not a uniform 200 metres

❗ **"NDZ equals 200 metres" is a simplification and it is wrong.** 📰 The instrument makes the no-development zone **conditional in four distinct ways**:

1. 📰 **CRZ-III B: 200 m** from HTL — the unconditional case, and the source of the simplification.
2. 📰 **CRZ-III A: 50 m** from HTL — ❗ **but only *"provided the CZMP… have been approved, failing which, a NDZ of 200 meters shall continue to apply."*** ⚠️ **The relaxation is earned by completing a planning process**, not granted by density alone. ❗ **A state that has not had its Coastal Zone Management Plan approved does not get the 50 m rule.**
3. 📰 **Along tidal-influenced water bodies in CRZ-III: 50 m or the width of the creek, whichever is less.**
4. 📰 **Verbatim: *"The NDZ shall not be applicable in the areas falling within notified Port limits."***

❗ **The examinable formulation:** ⚠️ *"The no-development zone is 200 m from the High Tide Line in CRZ-III B; 50 m in CRZ-III A **only where an approved Coastal Zone Management Plan exists**, failing which 200 m continues; 50 m or the creek width, whichever is less, along tidal-influenced water bodies; and it does not apply at all within notified Port limits."* ⚠️ That sentence contains four conditions and no error, which is more than most answers manage.

❗ **[N-19] And the instrument does not apply everywhere.** 📰 It expressly excludes *"the islands of Andaman and Nicobar and Lakshadweep and the marine areas surrounding these islands."* 🔁 **Those are governed by the Island Protection Zone Notification, 2011, which is Topic 11 territory — `Geography/basic/11`.** ❗ **"CRZ applies to the whole Indian coast" is false**, and C2's own trap list names the related error: *"CRZ rules apply uniformly to the whole coast → CRZ classifies the coast into categories with **different rules** for each."*

### 7. [N-20] The hazard line is a planning tool, not a setback

📰 **From Annexure-IV of the same gazette, the guidelines for preparing Coastal Zone Management Plans:** *"A 'Hazard line' has been demarcated by the **Survey of India (SOI)** taking into account the extent of the flooding on the land area due to water level fluctuations, **sea level rise and shoreline changes (erosion or accretion) occurring over a period of time**. The hazard line mapped by SOI has been **shared with the coastal States or Union territories through NCSCM**. The hazard line **shall be used as a tool for disaster management plan for the coastal environment, including planning of adaptive and mitigation measures**. With a view to reduce the vulnerability of the coastal communities and ensuring sustainable livelihood, while drawing the CZMP, **the land use planning for the area between the Hazard line and HTL shall take into account such impacts of climate change and shoreline changes**."*

❗ **Four things follow, and all four are correctives.**

1. ❗ **A different agency.** The hazard line is **Survey of India**; the HTL and LTL are **NCSCM**. ⚠️ Different line, different author.
2. ❗ **A different function.** It is *"a tool for disaster management plan"* and for **land-use planning**; it is **not** a no-development zone, **not** a universal setback and **not** a project-clearance boundary. ⚠️ ✅ C2 states this correctly and C1's terminology control names the confusion explicitly.
3. ❗ **A different input set.** It is built from **flooding extent, water-level fluctuation, sea-level rise and shoreline change over time** — which is to say, it is the one element of the instrument that is explicitly **dynamic and forward-looking**, where HTL and the fixed distances are static. 🔁 This is where L10, L11 and L15 enter the law.
4. ❗ **A different legal weight.** The NDZ prohibits; the hazard line **informs**.

📰 **The map apparatus, which makes the whole thing operable.** Coastal Zone Management Plans are prepared on **base maps of 1:25,000 scale** acquired from the Survey of India (1:50,000 enlarged where 1:25,000 is unavailable), on **7.5 minute × 7.5 minute** sheets, with **Everest or WGS 84** horizontal datum and **Mean Sea Level** vertical datum; and *"various regulatory lines viz. at a distance of **20 metres, 50 metres, 200 metres and 500 metres from HTL** respectively, as applicable in various CRZ categories, **and the Hazard line shall be demarcated and transferred to the CZM maps**."* ❗ **Four regulatory distances plus the hazard line, all on one 1:25,000 sheet** — that is what "coastal regulation" physically is.

🔁 **The routing that this lesson must make, by exact path, because the temptation here is at its maximum.** ❗ **CRZ clearance procedure, project appraisal, the Coastal Zone Management Authorities, State Environment Impact Assessment Authorities, permissible-activity schedules and compliance machinery are NOT taught here.** ⚠️ The repository's syllabus-mapping file states that **environmental-regulatory design is not claimed by Geography at all**. ❗ **They are owned by `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24_Coastal-and-Marine-Ecology-CRZ-Blue-Economy.md`**, together with **mangrove ecology and restoration policy including MISHTI**, **blue carbon**, **coastal sand mining**, **marine pollution** and ❗ **the 2022 GS-III Q18 coastal-erosion management demand**. ⚠️ **Topic 10 teaches the instrument as a map of the coast: where the lines are, what defines them, and what geographical facts they encode.**

### 8. 🇮🇳 What the instrument reveals about Indian coastal geography

⚠️ **Read as geography rather than as law, `G.S.R. 37(E)` is an unusually rich document, and this is the use an examiner can reward.**

- ⚠️ **It confirms that India's coast is defined by a tidal line, not by a shoreline.** ❗ Which is the whole of L1 and L15, written into law.
- ⚠️ **It recognises that creeks, estuaries, backwaters and lagoons are part of the coast** — the landforms of L6 and L7 — and gives them their own scaled rule.
- ⚠️ **It treats specific geomorphological features as coast-maintaining infrastructure:** mangroves, corals, **sand dunes**, biologically active mudflats, salt marshes. ❗ A **sand dune is listed as an ecologically sensitive area because it maintains the integrity of the coast** — which is precisely the dune-reservoir function taught at L7 and used at L17.
- ⚠️ **It encodes the sea-level and shoreline-change science of L10, L11 and L15 in a single line**, the hazard line, and requires land-use planning between that line and the HTL to account for climate change and shoreline change.
- ⚠️ **It names the coasts this edition has already taught** as critically vulnerable: the **Sundarban region**, the **Gulf of Khambat**, the **Gulf of Kutchh**, **Malvan and Achra-Ratnagiri**, **Karwar**.
- ❗ **And it excludes the islands**, which is a jurisdictional fact with a geographical cause: island coasts are governed by a separate instrument because they are a different coastal system. 🔁 `Geography/basic/11`.

❗ **The limitation, stated rather than skipped.** ⚠️ **No Coastal Zone Management Plan approval status for any Indian state was retrieved for this generation**, so ❗ **this edition states the 50 m rule's condition and does **not** say which states currently enjoy the relaxation. ⚠️ **No hazard-line map, no notified-port limit list and no CRZ area figure is asserted anywhere.** ❗ **U-01 stands: amendments exist and are not cited.**

### 9. Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What this lesson's claim explains | What it does not explain |
|---|---|---|
| ❗ The HWL/HTL separation | Why the 47.66 per cent longer coastline of 04 DEC 2025 changes no regulated area; why two agencies demarcate two lines | How either line is physically surveyed, which is a technique question |
| ⚠️ The zonation logic | Why the coast is divided by ecology, tide, built form, density and water; that the criteria are of four different kinds | Why the density threshold is 2161 per sq km on the 2011 base, which the instrument does not justify |
| ⚠️ The NDZ conditionality and the hazard line | Why "200 m" is wrong as a universal statement; why the hazard line informs rather than prohibits | Implementation, compliance and enforcement, all of which are routed out |

**Strongest objection.** ⚠️ This is environmental law. Geography has no business teaching a gazette notification clause by clause, and the repository's own audit classes these categories as skippable.

**Reply.** ⚠️ Three answers, and the third is decisive. **First**, the edition does **not** teach the instrument clause by clause: it teaches **two line definitions, one zonation logic, one conditional distance rule and one planning line**, and delivers the category enumeration as a **reference table that is explicitly not narrated** — which is exactly the resolution recorded at U-11. **Second**, the parts taught are **geographical in kind**: a tidal datum, a salinity criterion for tidal influence, a creek-width scaling rule, a feature-based ecological overlay and a forward-looking hazard line built from shoreline-change data. **Third, and decisively**, 📰 the single most valuable fact in this entire topic — that the **47.66 per cent** increase in measured coastline reported on **04 DEC 2025** leaves the regulated area untouched because **HWL is not HTL** — **cannot be stated at all without this lesson.** ❗ **Remove the lesson and the discriminator goes with it.** ⚠️ What Geography must not do is drift into **clearance procedure and institutional design**, and that boundary is drawn above by exact path.

❓ **Residual, which this edition does not close.** ❓ **The amendment history of CRZ 2019 is unverified in primary form and is not cited (U-01).** ❓ **No Coastal Zone Management Plan approval status is stated for any state**, so the practical reach of the 50 m rule is unknown to this edition. ❓ **The instrument does not explain the derivation of the 2161 per sq km threshold**, and this edition does not speculate. ❓ **No official statement reconciling the NCCR study length with the legacy administrative length was retrieved** (L15).

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** ⚠️ The *"changes in critical geographical features … and the effects of such changes"* clause (General Studies Paper I) — the hazard line **is** that clause operationalised — and the **PARTIAL, support-only** General Studies Paper III disaster clause, discharged here as the **planning instrument** without the institutional doctrine.
- **Verified PYQ:** ❗ **No verified CRZ question exists in the ledgers for any paper, 2018-2026, and none is invented.** ⚠️ C1's own PYQ-control clause records this. ⚠️ The lesson exists to make the owned demand **O2** answerable on its governance side and to carry the HWL/HTL discriminator.
- **Traps:** ⚠️ Citing the notification under the **S.O.** series — it is **`G.S.R. 37(E)`**; the S.O. series belongs to the superseded 2011 notification. ⚠️ Saying the NDZ is **200 m** without its four conditions. ⚠️ Treating the **hazard line** as a setback or clearance line. ⚠️ Saying CRZ covers the **whole** Indian coast — **A&N and Lakshadweep are excluded**. ⚠️ Confusing **HWL** with **HTL**. ⚠️ Giving an amendment number. ⚠️ Treating tidal influence as a distance when it is defined by **5 ppt salinity in the driest period**. ⚠️ Drifting into clearance procedure or authorities.
- **Answer use:** ❗ Answer a coastal-governance question with **lines first, categories second, conditions third**. ⚠️ *"Coastal regulation in India is anchored on one defined line — the High Tide Line, the spring-tide high-water line demarcated by NCSCM — from which the zone extends 500 m along the sea front and 50 m or the creek width along tidal-influenced water bodies, tidal influence itself being fixed by a 5 ppt salinity criterion in the driest season. Within that zone the coast is classified by ecological function, tidal position, built form, population density and water, with a no-development zone that is conditional rather than uniform; and a separate, forward-looking hazard line, demarcated by the Survey of India from flooding, sea-level-rise and shoreline-change data, informs land-use planning without prohibiting."*

Revision notes:

- ❌ **[N-01] The citation is `G.S.R. 37(E)`, 18 January 2019, Gazette of India Extraordinary, PART II—SEC. 3(i).** ❗ **Not an S.O.-series instrument** — that series letter belongs to the **superseded CRZ 2011, `S.O. 19(E)` of 6 January 2011**. ⚠️ Both issue under **section 3, Environment (Protection) Act, 1986**; the 2019 instrument follows the **Shailesh Nayak** committee report and a draft of **18 April 2018**.
- ❗ **[N-14] HWL ≠ HTL.** ⚠️ **HWL** measures (NHO + SoI, under NSCS terms of reference); **HTL** regulates (NCSCM, Chennai, under MoEFCC). 📰 The 11,098.81 km revision *"does not affect areas covered under the CRZ regulations"* (PIB 2198800, **04 DEC 2025**). ❗ **Coastline +47.66 %, regulated area unchanged.**
- 📰 **HTL defined:** *"the line on the land upto which the highest water line reaches during the **spring tide**"*, demarcated by **NCSCM**; ❗ *"only such demarcation of HTL and LTL shall be applicable for all purposes of this notification."*
- 📰 **Landward extent:** **HTL to 500 m** along the sea front; **HTL to 50 m or the creek width, whichever is less** along tidal-influenced water bodies — ⚠️ **100 m or creek width continues until the CZMP is approved**. 📰 **Tidal influence = salinity of five parts per thousand (ppt), measured in the DRIEST period of the year.** ❗ A **hydrological** criterion fixing a **legal** boundary.
- 📰 **Categories:** **I A** ESAs and coast-maintaining geomorphological features (**mangroves >1,000 sq m get a 50 m buffer**; corals, **sand dunes**, biologically active mudflats, protected areas under WLPA 1972 / FCA 1980 / EPA 1986 incl. Biosphere Reserves, salt marshes, turtle nesting grounds, **horse shoe crabs' habitats**, sea-grass beds, bird nesting grounds, archaeological and heritage sites) · **I B** intertidal zone (LTL–HTL) · **II** developed, within municipal/legally designated urban limits, **built-up ratio > 50 %** · **III A** density **> 2161 per sq km (2011 base)** · **III B** density **< 2161** · **IV A** LTL to **12 nautical miles** · **IV B** tidal-water-body water and bed.
- ⚠️ **The architecture mixes FOUR kinds of criterion** — ecological, tidal, built-form, demographic — plus hydrographic for water. ❗ **That observation beats reciting seven labels.**
- ❌ **[N-18] The NDZ is CONDITIONAL:** **200 m** in III B · **50 m in III A ONLY where an approved CZMP exists, failing which 200 m continues** · **50 m or creek width** along tidal water bodies in III · ❗ **not applicable at all within notified Port limits.**
- ❌ **[N-19] CRZ 2019 EXCLUDES the Andaman and Nicobar and Lakshadweep islands and their surrounding marine areas.** 🔁 Governed by the **Island Protection Zone Notification, 2011** → `Geography/basic/11`.
- ❌ **[N-20] The hazard line is a PLANNING tool.** 📰 Demarcated by the **Survey of India** from flooding extent, water-level fluctuation, **sea-level rise and shoreline change over time**; shared through NCSCM; *"shall be used as a tool for disaster management plan"*; land use **between the hazard line and HTL** must account for climate change and shoreline change. ❗ **Not an NDZ, not a setback, not a clearance line.**
- 📰 **CZMP apparatus:** base maps **1:25,000** from the Survey of India (1:50,000 enlarged if needed), **7.5′ × 7.5′** sheets, **Everest or WGS 84** horizontal datum, **Mean Sea Level** vertical datum; regulatory lines at **20 m, 50 m, 200 m and 500 m from HTL**, **plus the hazard line**, transferred to the maps. 📰 **Critically Vulnerable Coastal Areas named:** **Sundarban** region of West Bengal; **Gulf of Khambat** and **Gulf of Kutchh** in Gujarat; **Malvan, Achra-Ratnagiri** in Maharashtra; **Karwar**.
- 📰 **Mangrove linkage, one line (PIB 2146358, 21 JUL 2025):** ESA status with a **50 m buffer above 1,000 sq m**, and **three times the number of mangroves lost must be replanted**.
- ❗ **U-01 flagged:** **CRZ 2019 has been amended; this edition cites NO amendment number**, the parent being a G.S.R. and no amendment gazette having been retrieved in primary form. ❗ **U-11 resolved explicitly:** **zonation LOGIC is Core; category ENUMERATION is a reference table and is not narrated.**
- 🔁 **ROUTED OUT, by exact path:** **CRZ clearance procedure, appraisal, Coastal Zone Management Authorities, SEIAA machinery, permissible-activity schedules and compliance**, together with **mangrove ecology, MISHTI, blue carbon, sand mining, marine pollution and the 2022 GS-III Q18 erosion-management demand** → `Environment-and-Ecology/basic/24_Coastal-and-Marine-Ecology-CRZ-Blue-Economy.md`. ⚠️ The syllabus-mapping file states **environmental-regulatory design is not claimed by Geography at all**.
- ❗ **Limitation:** **no CZMP approval status, hazard-line map, notified-port list or CRZ area figure is asserted anywhere.**
- 🔑 **Mnemonic for the zone: HTL →500 · creek 50-or-width · LTL→12 Nm · 5 ppt driest · 2161 per sq km · >50 % built-up.**

### Concept check

**Question:** A planning official argues: "Since India's coastline has been officially revised upward by nearly 3,600 km, and since the no-development zone is 200 metres everywhere, the area under coastal regulation must have expanded by thousands of square kilometres." Identify every defect in the reasoning.

**Model answer:** The argument contains **three defects**, each independently fatal. First, it **conflates two different lines**. The upward revision to 11,098.81 km was produced by the National Hydrographic Office with the Survey of India using high-resolution **High-Water Line** data; the Coastal Regulation Zone is defined from the **High Tide Line**, the spring-tide high-water line demarcated by the National Centre for Sustainable Coastal Management, and the gazette provides that only that demarcation *"shall be applicable for all purposes of this notification."* The issuing ministry states the consequence expressly: the revision *"does not affect areas covered under the CRZ regulations"* and has *"no bearing on CRZ jurisdiction or its regulatory framework."* Second, the claim that **the no-development zone is "200 metres everywhere" is false**. It is 200 metres in CRZ-III B; it is 50 metres in CRZ-III A **but only where an approved Coastal Zone Management Plan exists, failing which 200 metres continues to apply**; it is 50 metres or the width of the creek, whichever is less, along tidal-influenced water bodies in CRZ-III; and it **does not apply at all within notified Port limits**. The zone is conditional, not uniform, and in CRZ-II — developed urban land — the no-development concept does not operate in the same way at all. Third, the argument **ignores the instrument's exclusions**: CRZ 2019 does not extend to the Andaman and Nicobar or Lakshadweep islands or their surrounding marine areas, which are governed separately, so even a genuine increase in measured coastline in those units could not expand this notification's reach. A corrected statement is that the regulated area is a function of the HTL demarcation, the category map and the conditional distances, and is unaffected by a change in how the national coastline is measured.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not assume that because two official lines both describe "where the sea meets the land", they are interchangeable or that a change to one propagates to the other. Measurement lines and regulatory lines are produced by different agencies, to different definitions, for different purposes, and the law names exactly one of them as operative.

**Original Mains practice [20 marks]:** Examine how India's coastal regulation translates coastal geomorphology into a legal boundary, and assess why a substantial upward revision of the national coastline length left the regulated area unchanged. *(Examine and assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model (about 250 words):** Coastal regulation must convert a zone into a line. The notification of 18 January 2019, `G.S.R. 37(E)`, does so by nominating the **High Tide Line** — the line reached by the highest water during the **spring tide**, demarcated by a single named agency, NCSCM — and measuring from it: **500 metres** landward along the sea front, and **50 metres or the creek width, whichever is less**, along tidal-influenced water bodies, tidal influence being fixed by a **salinity of five parts per thousand measured in the driest season**. The creek rule is a scale decision: a 500-metre band either side of a narrow creek would regulate a whole landscape.

The zonation encodes geomorphology directly. CRZ-I A protects features *"which play a role in maintaining the integrity of the coast"* — mangroves, corals, **sand dunes**, biologically active mudflats, salt marshes — treating landforms as coastal infrastructure. CRZ-I B is the intertidal band; CRZ-II and III divide land by built form and population density; CRZ-IV is water to twelve nautical miles. The no-development zone is conditional: 200 metres in III B, 50 metres in III A only where an approved management plan exists, and inapplicable within notified Port limits. A separate **hazard line**, demarcated by the Survey of India from flooding, sea-level-rise and shoreline-change data, informs planning without prohibiting.

The 2025 revision to 11,098.81 km used the **High-Water Line**. Because regulation rests solely on the HTL, the ministry confirmed it has no bearing on CRZ jurisdiction — two lines, two agencies, two purposes.

**Rubric (quantified, 20 marks):** 6 for the **HWL/HTL distinction**, requiring both agencies, both purposes and the ministry's own statement that the revision does not affect CRZ areas; 5 for the zonation, requiring the HTL-to-500 m and creek rules, the 5 ppt criterion and at least four categories correctly characterised, with the architecture recognised as mixing criteria of different kinds; 4 for NDZ conditionality, requiring at least three of the four conditions including the approved-plan proviso and the notified-port exclusion; 3 for the hazard line correctly placed as a Survey of India planning instrument built from shoreline-change and sea-level data, and explicitly not a setback; 2 for the exclusions, naming the Andaman and Nicobar and Lakshadweep exclusion and routing it to the island owner. Deduct 3 for citing the notification under the wrong gazette series, 3 for stating that the coastline revision expanded the regulated area, 2 for an unconditional 200 m no-development zone, and 2 for citing an amendment number that this edition does not verify.
